## Secondary Dominants: Function and Forward Motion

A **secondary dominant** is a dominant-quality chord (major triad or dominant 7th) built on a scale degree other than the fifth, temporarily tonicizing a diatonic chord that is not the tonic. In the key of C major, **V7/V = A7**, which treats the diatonic **Vmaj (G)** as a temporary tonic.

### Functional Role
Its function is **secondary dominant** — it borrows the *dominant function* of a non-home key center and applies it locally. It sits outside the parent key's diatonic collection but resolves *into* it, creating a **brief tonicization** without a full modulation.

### How It Creates Forward Motion
The mechanism is identical to a standard V7→I resolution, driven by two forces:

1. **Tritone tension** — the chord contains a tritone (in A7: C♯ and G) that demands resolution by half-step contrary motion (C♯→D, G→G, resolving into Gmaj7).
2. **Leading-tone pull** — the raised third of the secondary dominant acts as a chromatic leading tone to the root of the target chord, creating a *half-step approach* that the ear strongly anticipates.

The **chromatic alteration** (C♯ in the key of C) signals a departure from the diatonic norm, heightening tension, and the subsequent resolution to the expected diatonic chord releases that tension — producing a clear sense of **directed, purposeful motion**.
sonnet+Rule+RAGjudge 2/5
## Secondary Dominants: Function and Forward Motion

A **secondary dominant** is a dominant-quality chord (major triad or dominant 7th) built on a scale degree other than the fifth, temporarily tonicizing a diatonic chord that is not the tonic. The most common example, **V7/V**, is a dominant seventh chord whose root lies a perfect fifth above the V chord — in C major, that gives you **A7**, which resolves to **Dm7 (ii-7)** or **G7 (V7)**.

### Why It Creates Forward Motion

The mechanism is identical to a standard dominant resolution: the secondary dominant contains a **tritone** between its third and seventh (in A7: C♯ and G), which creates intense harmonic tension. The **leading tone** (C♯) has a strong half-step pull upward to D, while the **chordal seventh** (G) pulls downward by half-step — both resolving into the target chord. This is called **tritone resolution**. Additionally, the **root movement by descending perfect fifth** (A→D) reinforces the sense of arrival. Functionally, the secondary dominant acts as a **local dominant**, briefly suspending the main key center and granting the target chord a momentary sense of "I-ness," which propels the listener forward through a chain of tension-and-release events before ultimately returning to the primary tonic.
c06Why do Coltrane changes (Giant Steps) move by major thirds, and why are they hard to improvise over?

Key points (judge 기준):

  • the tonal centers cycle by major thirds (e.g. B–G–Eb), dividing the octave equally
  • each key center has its own ii-V or V-I, moving very fast
  • rapid key changes leave little time to outline each new tonality
  • requires memorized patterns / strong key-center awareness
haikurawjudge 4/5
# Coltrane Changes: Major Thirds and Difficulty

Coltrane chose major thirds (four semitones apart) because they divide the octave into three equal parts, creating a symmetrical harmonic structure that sounds exotic and unstable compared to traditional progressions. In "Giant Steps," the chords move C major → E major → Ab major → back to C, giving equal weight to all three tonal centers rather than establishing one home key.

This makes improvisation challenging for several reasons: First, there's no clear tonal hierarchy—your ear can't easily predict where the harmony is going. Second, the rapid chord changes (often every two beats) demand quick mental processing and fingering shifts. Third, traditional scale-based soloing breaks down because no single scale fits all three keys equally well; you must either think in three separate tonalities simultaneously or use chromatic/symmetrical approaches like diminished or whole-tone scales. Finally, the lack of functional harmony (no dominant-to-tonic resolution) removes the harmonic landmarks that guide most jazz improvisers, requiring instead a more abstract, intervallic approach to navigation.
haiku+Rulejudge 5/5
# Coltrane Changes: Structure and Challenge

**Why major thirds?** Giant Steps cycles through three tonal centers a major third apart (B, G, Eb), creating a symmetrical harmonic structure. Each center gets a ii-V-I progression: **ii-7 | V7 | Imaj7**. In B: C#-7 | F#7 | Bmaj7; in G: A-7 | D7 | Gmaj7; in Eb: F-7 | Bb7 | Ebmaj7. The major-third transposition (12 semitones ÷ 3 = 4-semitone intervals) divides the octave symmetrically, creating harmonic instability—no single tonal center dominates, so the ear never fully "lands."

**Why difficult to improvise?** The rapid harmonic rhythm (one chord per beat or two) and constant key shifts demand:
1. **Scalar fluency**: You must instantly access the correct mode for each chord (Dorian for ii-7, altered or Phrygian dominant for V7, Ionian for Imaj7) across three unrelated keys
2. **Harmonic thinking over linear thinking**: Chord-tone soloing and arpeggiation become essential; scalar runs risk clashing with the shifting harmonic ground
3. **No tonal anchor**: Unlike standard changes where V7 pulls to I, here each resolution is immediately displaced by a new key area, preventing the relaxation that guides phrasing

Mastering Giant Steps requires internalizing all three keys simultaneously and thinking in short, deliberate harmonic units rather than long melodic lines.

# Coltrane Changes: Structure and Challenge

**Why major thirds?** Giant Steps cycles through three tonal centers a major third apart (B, G, Eb), creating a symmetrical harmonic architecture. Each center gets a ii-V-I progression: B major (C#-7, F#7, Bmaj7), G major (A-7, D7, Gmaj7), Eb major (F-7, Bb7, Ebmaj7). This equal division of the octave (12 semitones ÷ 3 = 4 semitones per step) maximizes harmonic distance and avoids a single gravitational center—a radical departure from functional tonality.

**Why difficult to improvise?** The rapid modulation (every 2 bars) gives improvisers no stable tonal home. Each V7 is a secondary dominant pulling toward its local I, but the next chord immediately shifts the key center. A soloist must either: (1) outline each chord's arpeggio precisely, (2) use chromatic or symmetrical scales (diminished, whole-tone) that work across all three keys, or (3) think in tritone substitutions and chromatic voice-leading. Traditional scalar improvisation (playing in one mode) fails because no single scale fits all three centers. This demands harmonic sophistication and rapid mental recalibration—hence its legendary difficulty.

Coltrane changes divide the octave into three equal parts by moving through key centers a major third apart — in Giant Steps, that's B major, G major, and E♭ major. Since three major thirds stack up to exactly 12 semitones (4+4+4), these three tonal centers perfectly partition the octave, creating a symmetrical harmonic system. Coltrane used ii-V-I progressions to connect these centers, so the changes move through keys very rapidly — often cycling through all three tonal centers in just two bars. This is what makes them so difficult to improvise over: the key centers shift every one or two beats, giving you almost no time to establish yourself in any one tonality before the harmony yanks you somewhere else. Traditional bebop vocabulary, which tends to linger in a key and navigate chord changes more gradually, doesn't map cleanly onto this structure. Improvisers have to either think very quickly in terms of arpeggios and scales for each fleeting chord, or find melodic shapes and patterns that work across all three tonal centers simultaneously — which requires deep internalization of the symmetrical structure rather than conventional tonal thinking.

## Coltrane Changes: Major-Third Root Motion

**Why major thirds?** Coltrane divided the octave into three **equal parts** — B, G, and E♭ — each a major third (4 semitones) apart. Three major thirds = 12 semitones = one octave, creating a **symmetrical tonal cycle** that replaces the single ii–V–I with three interlocking ii–V–I cells, each a major third away. In *Giant Steps*, the opening progression reads:

**Bmaj7 → D7 → Gmaj7 → B♭7 → E♭maj7 → A-7 → D7 → Gmaj7**

Formally: **Imaj7 / V7/III → IIImaj7 / V7/♭VII → ♭VIImaj7** — each dominant functions as a **secondary dominant** pivoting to the next tonic a major third below, cycling through all three tonal centers (B, G, E♭).

**Why hard to improvise over?** Three compounding reasons:

1. **Rapid harmonic rhythm** — chords change every one or two beats, leaving almost no time to establish a tonal center before it vanishes
2. **No single parent scale** — each ii–V–I cell demands its own **Dorian (2nd mode of major)**, **Mixolydian (5th mode of major)**, and **Ionian** vocabulary; there is no overarching key to "stay in"
3. **Symmetry resists melodic logic** — because the three key centers are equidistant, conventional tonal gravity (dominant → tonic resolution) feels equally strong in all three directions simultaneously

## Coltrane Changes: Major-Third Root Motion and Improvisational Challenges

### Why Major Thirds?

Coltrane partitions the octave into **three equal divisions of a major third (4 semitones)**, exploiting the symmetry of the augmented triad: **B – G – E♭ (= D#)**. Each tonal center is a major third apart, so three II–V–I cycles exhaust all three key centers and return to the origin after one octave. In *Giant Steps*, the three tonic centers are **B major, G major, and E♭ major**, each approached by its own V7. The root motion (B→G→E♭→B) is **tonally symmetric**, meaning no single center is privileged — the "home" key of B major is structurally equivalent to the other two. This is sometimes called a **chromatic mediant substitution system**, where each V7 resolves down a fifth *within* a rapidly rotating tonal axis rather than prolonging a single tonic.

| 2 | **Gmaj7** | **Imaj7** (G) | Tonic |

c07Explain the difference between a ii-V-i in a minor key vs a ii-V-I in a major key (chord qualities and scales).

Key points (judge 기준):

  • minor: iiø7 (half-diminished), V7(b9/alt), i-(minor)
  • major: ii-7, V7, Imaj7
  • minor V often uses harmonic minor / altered; major V uses mixolydian
  • the ii in minor is m7b5 vs m7 in major
haikurawjudge 5/5
# ii-V-i vs ii-V-I: Minor vs Major

In a **minor key (ii-V-i)**, the progression uses: ii°7b5 (half-diminished) → V7alt (dominant with alterations) → i (minor). The ii°7b5 comes from the harmonic minor scale and contains the characteristic tritone with the V. Over this progression, you'd typically use **harmonic minor** for the i chord, **Phrygian dominant** or **altered scale** over the V7, and **Dorian or harmonic minor** over the ii°7b5.

In a **major key (ii-V-I)**, you get: ii7 (minor 7th) → V7 (dominant 7th) → I (major). Both chords derive naturally from the major scale. You'd use **major scale** over the I and ii7, and **mixolydian** over the V7 (or the major scale itself). The ii7 is diatonic and less tense than the diminished ii°7b5, making the major progression feel more stable and resolved.

**Key difference**: The minor version has darker, more exotic harmony (the half-diminished chord and harmonic minor flavor), while the major version is brighter and more grounded in diatonic harmony.
haiku+Rulejudge 5/5
# ii-V-i (Minor) vs ii-V-I (Major)

**Minor key (ii-V-i):** The progression uses iiø7-V7-i. The ii chord is a **half-diminished seventh** (minor-7♭5) because it's built on scale degree 2 of natural minor, giving it a diminished triad with a minor seventh. The V7 is a **dominant seventh** (major triad + minor seventh), typically drawn from harmonic minor to create the leading tone. The i is a **minor triad**. Functionally: iiø7 acts as subdominant, V7 as dominant, i as tonic. The V7 often uses the **altered scale** (7th mode of melodic minor) or **harmonic minor** to accommodate the raised 7th scale degree.

**Major key (ii-V-I):** The progression uses ii-7-V7-Imaj7. The ii chord is a **minor seventh** (minor triad + minor seventh) built on scale degree 2 of major. The V7 is identical in quality but typically uses the **mixolydian mode** (5th mode of major) or **altered scale** for color. The I is a **major triad** or **major seventh**. Functionally identical: ii-7 (subdominant), V7 (dominant), I (tonic).

**Key difference:** The ii chord quality changes—**iiø7 in minor vs ii-7 in major**—because scale degree 2 sits above a diminished triad in minor but above a major triad in major.
haiku+Rule+RAGjudge 5/5
# Minor ii-V-i vs. Major ii-V-I

**Local key center first:** In C minor, the progression is ii–V–i; in C major, it's ii–V–I.

**Chord qualities and scales:**

In **C major ii-V-I**: Dm7–G7–Cmaj7, all derived from the C major scale. The ii is minor-7, the V is dominant-7 (with natural 9 and 13), and the i resolves to a major-7 chord.

In **C minor ii-V-i**: Dm7♭5–G7♭9–Cm7, derived from C harmonic minor. The ii is *half-diminished* (iiø7) because the 5th is lowered, the V is dominant-7 *with a ♭9* (from harmonic minor's ♭6), and the i resolves to a minor-7 chord. That ♭9 on the V is the decisive sonic marker—it creates the darker, stronger pull characteristic of minor tonality and makes altered/diminished sounds natural over the dominant.

**In short:** The scale foundation (major vs. harmonic minor) determines everything: major uses natural extensions; minor uses ♭5 on the ii and ♭9 on the V, giving it a distinctly darker character.

## ii-V-I in Major vs ii-V-i in Minor

In a **major key**, the ii-V-I uses a **minor 7th chord** (ii⁷), a **dominant 7th chord** (V⁷), and a **major 7th chord** (Imaj7) — for example, in C major: Dm7 - G7 - Cmaj7. The corresponding scales are **Dorian** over the ii chord, **Mixolydian** over the V chord, and **Ionian (major scale)** over the I chord. In a **minor key**, the ii chord becomes a **half-diminished chord** (iiø7, also called a minor 7♭5), the V chord is still a **dominant 7th** (V7) — but now with a raised 7th degree borrowed from the harmonic minor scale to create that leading tone — and the i chord is a **minor major 7th or minor 7th chord**. In C minor, this gives you: Dm7♭5 - G7 - Cm. The scales shift accordingly: **Locrian** (or Locrian ♮2) over the iiø7, **Phrygian Dominant** (5th mode of harmonic minor) over the V7, and **harmonic or melodic minor** over the i chord. The half-diminished ii chord and the altered dominant are the key sonic markers that signal a minor key resolution.

## Minor ii-V-i vs. Major ii-V-I

**Local key centers and chord qualities:**

In a **major key** (e.g., C major), the cadence is **ii-7 — V7 — Imaj7**: the ii chord is a minor 7th (Dm7), the V is a dominant 7th with a natural 9 (G7), and the I resolves to a major 7th chord (Cmaj7). The entire progression derives from the **Ionian (major) scale**; the ii chord uses Dorian (2nd mode), and the V chord is most naturally approached with Mixolydian or Lydian dominant.

In a **minor key** (e.g., C minor), the cadence is **iiø7 — V7(♭9) — i-7**: the ii chord is a **half-diminished** (Dm7♭5 = Dø7), the V is still a dominant 7th but now carries a **♭9** (and typically ♭13) — G7(♭9,♭13) — and the i resolves to a **minor 7th** chord (Cm7). This progression derives from **harmonic minor** (C harmonic minor); the iiø7 uses **Locrian ♮2** (6th mode of harmonic minor), and the V7(♭9) uses the **7th mode of harmonic minor (= altered/Phrygian dominant)**, which supplies the ♭9 and ♭13 naturally from the ♭6 scale degree.

**The decisive differences:** the ii chord quality (minor 7th vs. half-diminished) and the V chord's
c08Explain tension and resolution in a dominant 7th chord using the tritone between its 3rd and 7th.

Key points (judge 기준):

  • the 3rd and 7th of a dominant form a tritone (unstable interval)
  • e.g. in G7 the tritone is B–F
  • the tritone resolves inward/outward to the tonic chord tones (B→C, F→E for C)
  • this drives the V→I resolution
haikurawjudge 5/5
# Tension and Resolution in the Dominant 7th

The dominant 7th chord (V7) creates tension primarily through the **tritone interval** between its 3rd and 7th scale degrees. For example, in C major, a G7 chord contains B (the 3rd) and F (the 7th)—a tritone apart. This interval is inherently unstable and dissonant, creating a strong pull toward resolution. The tritone *wants* to resolve inward: the B naturally rises to C, while the F naturally falls to E, both moving to chord tones of the I chord (C major). This voice-leading resolution is so powerful that it defines functional harmony itself. The tritone's tension makes V7→I feel inevitable and satisfying, which is why this cadence is the most fundamental harmonic movement in jazz and classical music. Without this tritone tension, the V7 would lack its characteristic urgency and wouldn't drive toward resolution as compellingly.
haiku+Rulejudge 5/5
# Tension and Resolution in the Dominant 7th Tritone

A dominant 7th chord (V7) contains a tritone between its 3rd and 7th scale degrees—for example, in C major, G7 has B (3rd) and F (7th), which are six semitones apart. This interval is acoustically unstable and creates maximum harmonic tension because the two notes are equidistant from any tonal center, making the ear crave resolution.

**Resolution mechanism:** The tritone resolves by contrary motion—the 3rd (B) pulls upward to the tonic (C), while the 7th (F) pulls downward to the 3rd of the tonic chord (E). This inward resolution to the Imaj7 (or i in minor) satisfies the tension because each note moves by half-step to a stable chord tone. The tritone's instability is thus the *functional engine* of V7→I cadences: without this dissonance, the progression lacks the gravitational pull that defines tonal harmony. This is why V7 is the dominant function—it creates directed, irreversible tension that demands tonic resolution.
haiku+Rule+RAGjudge 1/5
# Tension and Resolution in the Dominant 7th: The Tritone

The tritone (augmented 4th / diminished 5th) between the 3rd and 7th of a dominant 7 chord is the engine of its tension. In C7, that's E–B♭, an interval that divides the octave exactly in half and is acoustically unstable—it "wants" to resolve. In functional harmony, E (the 3rd of C7) naturally pulls up by semitone to the root of the target chord, while B♭ (the 7th) pulls down by semitone to the 3rd of that chord. When C7 resolves to F major, E→F and B♭→E create smooth, stepwise voice-leading that releases the tritone's tension. This pull-and-release cycle is what makes V7→I (or V7→vi) so powerful: the tritone's dissonance *demands* resolution, making the dominant 7 the most functionally directive chord in tonal music. Without that tritone, a major 7 chord (which has a perfect 5th and major 7th—both consonant intervals) feels complete and stable, requiring no onward motion.
sonnetrawjudge 5/5
The dominant 7th chord is a powerful tension-creating device largely because of the **tritone** — an interval of an augmented 4th or diminished 5th — that exists between its 3rd and 7th. In a G7 chord, for example, the 3rd (B) and the 7th (F) form this tritone, which is the most dissonant interval in tonal music. These two notes have a strong *tendency* to resolve inward or outward by half step: the B wants to resolve *up* to C (the root of the tonic chord), while the F wants to resolve *down* to E (the 3rd of the tonic chord). This contrary motion pulls the listener's ear toward the tonic chord — in this case, C major — creating a satisfying sense of arrival and rest. The tritone essentially acts like a coiled spring; the harmonic tension it generates makes resolution feel not just pleasant but almost *inevitable*, which is why the V7–I progression is the most fundamental and powerful cadential motion in tonal music.
sonnet+Rulejudge 5/5
## Tension and Resolution in the Dominant 7th Chord

In a **G7 chord** (functioning as V7 in C major), the defining tension lives in the **tritone interval between the 3rd and 7th**: **B (major 3rd) and F (minor 7th)**. These two pitches are separated by an augmented 4th / diminished 5th — the most dissonant diatonic interval — and each carries a strong **melodic obligation**. The **B** (leading tone, scale degree 7̂) has an upward half-step pull to **C** (the tonic, scale degree 1̂), while the **F** (chordal 7th, scale degree 4̂) has a downward half-step pull to **E** (the major 3rd of Imaj7, scale degree 3̂). When G7 resolves to **Cmaj7 (Imaj7)**, both voices move **inward by half step simultaneously**, collapsing the tritone into a **major 3rd (C–E)** — a stable, consonant interval at the heart of the tonic chord. This **contrary-motion resolution of the tritone** is the engine of functional harmony: it is precisely why the V7→I motion feels inevitable, and why any chord sharing this same tritone — such as the **subV7 (D♭7)**, which contains the enharmonically equivalent B♭/C♭ and F — can substitute for G7 and resolve to the same tonic with equal (or greater) chromatic force.
sonnet+Rule+RAGjudge 5/5
## Tension and Resolution: The Tritone Inside the Dominant 7th

The dominant 7th chord's restless energy comes almost entirely from a single interval hiding inside it: the **tritone** — an augmented 4th / diminished 5th spanning exactly six semitones, the most dissonant interval in tonal music.

In **G7** (the V7 in C major), the tritone sits between **B (the 3rd)** and **F (the 7th)**. These two notes are under enormous acoustic pressure to move: B wants to resolve **upward by a half step to C** (the tonic), and F wants to resolve **downward by a half step to E** (the major 3rd of the tonic). This contrary, inward motion is called **tritone resolution**. When G7 moves to Cmaj7, both voices collapse inward — B→C and F→E — and the tritone dissolves into a stable major 3rd. That release of tension *is* the sensation of resolution. Functionally, G7 carries **dominant function** precisely because of this tritone; the chord is essentially *defined* by its need to resolve to the tonic. This also explains why the **altered scale** (7th mode of melodic minor) and **Mixolydian** are the primary scale choices over V7 — both preserve that critical B and F, keeping the tritone, and therefore the dominant function, fully intact.

haiku+Rule Plagal Cadence (또는 Amen Cadence). IV에서 I로의 진행은 전통적 재즈 하모니에서 plagal cadence로 분류되며, 이는 subdominant에서 tonic으로의 해결을 나타냅니다. Oscar Peterson의 Hymn to Freedom에서 이 진행은 영가(spiritual) 전통의 종교적 음향을 강조합니다. ✓
haiku+Rule+RAG Plagal cadence (IV–I), 또는 'Amen cadence'라고 불립니다. 교회 음악에서 유래한 이 케이던스는 subdominant에서 tonic으로 진행하는 특징적인 종지형입니다. ✓
sonnetraw plagal cadence (플라갈 케이던스) ✓
sonnet+Rule plagal cadence (플라갈 케이던스). 기능적으로 IV(subdominant) → I(tonic)의 진행으로, 'Amen cadence'라고도 불린다. Bb장조 기준으로 IVmaj(EbMaj) → Imaj(BbMaj)의 subdominant→tonic 해결이다. ✓
sonnet+Rule+RAG 표절 케이던스(Plagal Cadence), 즉 IV→I 진행으로, 교회 음악에서 '아멘(Amen)' 케이던스라고도 불린다. ✓
e07 Kenny Dorham의 Blue Bossa는 어느 키로 시작하는가?

Accept: C minor / Cm / C- / C 마이너 / C minor key / Cmin / C 단조

조건답채점
haikuraw C 메이저 키 ✓
haiku+Rule C major (또는 C Ionian). Blue Bossa는 전통적으로 C major 키로 시작하며, 첫 번째 코드는 Cmaj7 (Imaj7)입니다. ✓
haiku+Rule+RAG C 마이너(C minor). Blue Bossa는 Cm7으로 시작하며, 홈 키는 C 마이너이다. 곡의 후반부에서 ♭II 메이저(D♭ 메이저)로 잠시 전조했다가 다시 C 마이너로 복귀하는 구조를 가진다. ✓
sonnetraw C단조 ✓
sonnet+Rule C단조(C minor)로 시작합니다. 첫 번째 섹션은 Cm7(i-7) → Fm7(iv-7) → Dm7b5(iiø7) → G7(V7) → Cm7(i-7)의 진행으로 C Dorian/C minor 토닉 센터를 확립합니다. ✓
sonnet+Rule+RAG C 마이너(C minor)로 시작한다. ✓

e10 Body and Soul (Db major)은 1도(I)가 아닌 어떤 도수(roman numeral)의 코드로 시작하는가?

Accept: ii / ii도 / ii chord / ii-7 / 2도 / Ebm7 / E♭m7 / 2

조건답채점
haikuraw vi (F minor) ✗
haiku+Rule Dbmaj7은 vi-7(b5) / Bbm7(b5)로 시작합니다. Db 장조에서 vi도수는 Bbm7(b5) (반감7화음)이며, 이는 상대 단조인 Bbm의 ii-7(b5)에 해당합니다. Body and Soul은 관례적으로 vi-7(b5)에서 시작하는 전형적인 표준곡입니다. ✓
haiku+Rule+RAG ```json { "answer": "ii (Ebm7) — Body and Soul은 D♭ major에서 I(DbMaj7)이 아닌 ii(Ebm7)로 시작한다. 이는 지연된 토닉 오프닝으로, 청자를 즉시 홈으로 안내하지 않고 ii-V-I 진행을 통해 토닉을 향해 끌어당기는 효과를 만든다. 이러한 구조가 이 발라드에 ✓
sonnetraw II7 (IIm7 또는 Ebm7, 즉 Db장조의 ii7 코드) ✓

sonnet+Rule+RAG ii (Ebm7) ✓
